## Demographics

Main article: [Demographics of Louisiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_Louisiana)

The majority of the state's population lives in southern Louisiana, spread throughout [Greater New Orleans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Orleans_metropolitan_area), the [Florida Parishes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Florida_Parishes), and [Acadiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acadiana),[^cite_note-153][^cite_note-\:3-154][^cite_note-155] while [Central](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Louisiana) and [North Louisiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Louisiana) have been stagnating and losing population.[^cite_note-156] From the [2020 U.S. census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_United_States_census), Louisiana had an apportioned population of 4,661,468.[^cite_note-157][^cite_note-158][^cite_note-159] Its resident population was 4,657,757 as of 2020.[^cite_note-160] In 2010, the state of Louisiana had a population of 4,533,372, up from 76,556 in [1810](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1810_United_States_census).&#x20;

Despite historically positive trends of population growth leading up to the 2020 census, Louisiana began to experience population decline and stagnation since 2021, with [Southwest Louisiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southwest_Louisiana)'s Calcasieu and Cameron parishes losing more than 5% of their populations individually.[^cite_note-161] Experiencing decline due to deaths and emigration to other states outpacing births and in-migration,[^cite_note-162][^cite_note-163][^cite_note-164][^cite_note-USCensusEst2022-165] Louisiana's 2022 census-estimated population was 4,590,241.[^cite_note-166]&#x20;

According to [immigration](15051.md) statistics in 2019, approximately 4.2% of Louisianians were immigrants, while 2% were native-born U.S. citizens with at least one immigrant parent. The majority of Louisianian immigrants came from Honduras (18.8%), Mexico (13.6%), Vietnam (11.3%), Cuba (5.8%), and India (4.4%); an estimated 29.4% were undocumented immigrants.[^cite_note-\:2-167] Its documented and undocumented population collectively paid \$1.2 billion in taxes.[^cite_note-\:2-167] New Orleans has been defined as a [sanctuary city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanctuary_city).[^cite_note-168][^cite_note-169][^cite_note-170]&#x20;

The population density of the state is 104.9 people per square mile.[^cite_note-2010.census.gov-171] The [center of population](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Center_of_population) of Louisiana is located in [Pointe Coupee Parish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pointe_Coupee_Parish,_Louisiana), in the city of [New Roads](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Roads,_Louisiana).[^cite_note-172] According to [HUD](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Department_of_Housing_and_Urban_Development)'s 2022 [Annual Homeless Assessment Report](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Annual_Homeless_Assessment_Report_to_Congress), there were an estimated 7,373 [homeless](19394651.md) people in Louisiana.[^cite_note-173][^cite_note-174]&#x20;

In 2022, Louisiana had the highest percent of births to unmarried women of any US state, at 54.7 percent.[^cite_note-175]&#x20;

Several [American Indian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Native_Americans_in_the_United_States) tribes such as the [Atakapa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atakapa) and [Caddo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caddo) inhabited Louisiana before European colonization, concentrated along the [Red River](194242.md) and Gulf of Mexico.[^cite_note-185][^cite_note-186][^cite_note-187][^cite_note-188] At the beginning of French and Spanish colonization of Louisiana, [white](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Americans) and [black Americans](2154.md) began to move into the area.[^cite_note-189][^cite_note-190] From French and Spanish rule in Louisiana, they were joined by [Filipinos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filipino_Americans), [Germans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_Americans), and [Spaniards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_Americans) both slave and free, who settled in enclaves within the Greater New Orleans region and Acadiana;[^cite_note-191][^cite_note-192][^cite_note-193][^cite_note-194] some of the Spanish-descended communities became the [Isleños](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isle%C3%B1os_\(Louisiana\)) of [St. Bernard Parish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Bernard_Parish,_Louisiana).[^cite_note-\:9-56]&#x20;

By the 19th and 20th centuries, the state's most-populous racial and ethnic group fluctuated between white and black Americans; 47% of the population was black or African American in 1900.[^cite_note-195] The [black Louisianian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_Americans_in_Louisiana) population declined following migration to states including New York and California in efforts to flee Jim Crow regulations.[^cite_note-196]&#x20;

At the end of the 20th century, Louisiana's population has experienced diversification again, and its [non-Hispanic or non-Latino American white](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-Hispanic_or_Latino_whites) population has been declining.[^cite_note-\:3-154] Since 2020, the black or African American population have made up the largest non-white share of youths.[^cite_note-197] [Hispanic and Latino Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hispanic_and_Latino_Americans) have also increased as the second-largest racial and ethnic composition in the state, making up nearly 7% of Louisiana's population at the 2020 census.[^cite_note-\:3-154] As of 2018,[^cite_note-\:1-198] the largest single Hispanic and Latino American ethnicity were [Mexican Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexican_Americans) (2.0%), followed by [Puerto Ricans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puerto_Ricans) (0.3%) and [Cuban Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuban_Americans) (0.2%). Other Hispanic and Latino Americans altogether made up 2.6% of Louisiana's Hispanic or Latino American population. The [Asian American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asian_Americans) and [multiracial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multiracial_Americans) communities have also experienced rapid growth,[^cite_note-\:3-154] with many of Louisiana's multiracial population identifying as [Cajun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cajuns) or [Louisiana Creole](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louisiana_Creole_people).[^cite_note-199]&#x20;

At the 2019 [American Community Survey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Community_Survey), the largest ancestry groups of Louisiana were African American (31.4%), [French](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Americans) (9.6%), German (6.2%), [English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_Americans) (4.6%), [Italian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italian_Americans) (4.2%), and [Scottish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_Americans) (0.9%).[^cite_note-200] African American and French heritage have been dominant since colonial Louisiana. As of 2011, 49.0% of Louisiana's population younger than age 1 were minorities.[^cite_note-201]&#x20;

### Religion

| Religion |  | Percent |
| --- | --- | --- |
| [Protestant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestant) |  | 53% |
| [Catholic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic) |  | 22% |
| Other Christian |  | 1.5% |
| [Unaffiliated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irreligion) |  | 19% |
| Jewish |  | 1% |
| Other faith |  | 4% |

As an ethnically and culturally diverse state, pre-colonial, colonial and present-day Louisianians have adhered to a variety of religions and spiritual traditions; pre-colonial and colonial Louisianian peoples practiced various [Native American religions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Native_American_religions) alongside [Christianity](5211.md) through the establishment of [Spanish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_missions_in_Louisiana) and [French missions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_colonization_of_the_Americas);[^cite_note-\:8-203] and other faiths including [Haitian Vodou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haitian_Vodou) and [Louisiana Voodoo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louisiana_Voodoo) were introduced to the state and are practiced to the present day.[^cite_note-204] In the colonial and present-day [U.S. state](18618239.md) of Louisiana, Christianity grew to become its predominant religion, representing 84% of the adult population in 2014 and 76.5% in 2020,[^cite_note-\:6-205][^cite_note-PRRI2020-202] during two separate studies by the [Pew Research Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pew_Research_Center) and [Public Religion Research Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_Religion_Research_Institute).&#x20;



[Antioch Baptist Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antioch_Baptist_Church_\(Shreveport,_Louisiana\)) in Shreveport

Among its Christian population—and in common with other southern U.S. states—the majority, particularly in the north of the state, belong to various Protestant denominations. [Protestantism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestantism) was introduced to the state in the 1800s, with Baptists establishing two churches in 1812, followed by Methodists; Episcopalians first entered the state by 1805.[^cite_note-206] Protestant Christians made up 57% of the state's adult population at the 2014 Pew Research Center study, and 53% at the 2020 Public Religion Research Institute's study. Protestants are concentrated in North Louisiana, Central Louisiana, and the northern tier of the Florida Parishes.&#x20;

&#x20;Because of French and Spanish heritage, and their descendants the Creoles, and later Irish, Italian, Portuguese and German immigrants, southern Louisiana and Greater New Orleans are predominantly Catholic in contrast; according to the 2020 Public Religion Research Institute study, 22% of the adult population were Catholic.[^cite_note-PRRI2020-202] Since Creoles were the first settlers, planters and leaders of the territory, they have traditionally been well represented in politics; for instance, most of the early governors were Creole Catholics, instead of Protestants.[^cite_note-\:8-203] As Catholics continue to constitute a significant fraction of Louisiana's population, they have continued to be influential in state politics. The high proportion and influence of the Catholic population makes Louisiana distinct among southern states.[^cite_note-207] The [Roman Catholic Archdiocese of New Orleans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Archdiocese_of_New_Orleans), [Diocese of Baton Rouge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Diocese_of_Baton_Rouge), and [Diocese of Lafayette in Louisiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Diocese_of_Lafayette_in_Louisiana) are the largest Catholic jurisdictions in the state, located within the Greater New Orleans, Greater Baton Rouge, and Lafayette metropolitan statistical areas.



[Cathedral Basilica of St. Louis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Louis_Cathedral_\(New_Orleans\)) in New Orleans

Louisiana was among the southern states with a significant Jewish population before the 20th century; Virginia, South Carolina, and Georgia also had influential Jewish populations in some of their major cities from the 18th and 19th centuries. The earliest Jewish colonists were [Sephardic Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sephardic_Jews) who immigrated to the [Thirteen Colonies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thirteen_Colonies). Later in the 19th century, German Jews began to immigrate, followed by those from eastern Europe and the Russian Empire in the late 19th and early 20th centuries. Jewish communities have been established in the state's larger cities, notably New Orleans and Baton Rouge.[^cite_note-208][^cite_note-209] The most significant of these is the Jewish community of the New Orleans area. In 2000, before the 2005 Hurricane Katrina, its population was about 12,000. Dominant Jewish movements in the state include [Orthodox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orthodox_Judaism) and [Reform Judaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reform_Judaism); Reform Judaism was the largest Jewish tradition in the state according to the Association of Religion Data Archives in 2020, representing some 5,891 Jews.[^cite_note-\:22-210] Prominent Jews in Louisiana's political leadership have included Whig (later Democrat) [Judah P. Benjamin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judah_P._Benjamin), who represented Louisiana in the [U.S. Senate](24909346.md) before the [American Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Civil_War) and then became the [Confederate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confederate_States_of_America) secretary of state; Democrat-turned-Republican [Michael Hahn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Hahn) who was elected as governor, serving 1864–1865 when Louisiana was occupied by the Union Army, and later elected in 1884 as a U.S. congressman;[^cite_note-211] Democrat [Adolph Meyer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adolph_Meyer), [Confederate Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confederate_Army) officer who represented the state in the [U.S. House of Representatives](19468510.md) from 1891 until his death in 1908; [Republican](32070.md) [secretary of state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secretary_of_State_\(U.S._state_government\)) [Jay Dardenne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jay_Dardenne), and Republican ([Democrat](5043544.md) before 2011) [attorney general](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Attorneys_General_of_Louisiana) [Buddy Caldwell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddy_Caldwell).&#x20;

Other non-Christian and non-Jewish religions with a continuous, historical presence in the state have been [Islam](6037917.md), [Buddhism](3267529.md) and [Hinduism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hinduism). In the [Shreveport–Bossier City metropolitan area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shreveport%E2%80%93Bossier_City_metropolitan_area), Muslims made up an estimated 14% of Louisiana's total Muslim population as of 2014.[^cite_note-212] In 2020, the Association of Religion Data Archives estimated there were 24,732 Muslims living in the state.[^cite_note-\:22-210] The largest Islamic denominations in the major metropolises of Louisiana were [Sunni Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunni_Islam), [non-denominational Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-denominational_Muslim) and [Quranism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quranism), [Shia Islam](26961.md), and the [Nation of Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nation_of_Islam).[^cite_note-213]&#x20;

Among Louisiana's irreligious community, 2% affiliated with [atheism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atheism) and 13% claimed no religion as of 2014; an estimated 10% of the state's population practiced nothing in particular at the 2014 study. According to the Public Religion Research Institute in 2020, 19% were religiously unaffiliated.[^cite_note-PRRI2020-202]&#x20;

## Economy

See also: [Louisiana locations by per capita income](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louisiana_locations_by_per_capita_income) and [Agriculture in Louisiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agriculture_in_Louisiana)



A cargo ship at the [Port of New Orleans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_New_Orleans)

Louisiana's population, [agricultural products](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agricultural_productivity), abundance of [oil and natural gas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oil_and_gas_law_in_the_United_States), and southern Louisiana's medical and technology corridors have contributed to its growing and diversifying economy.[^cite_note-214] In 2014, Louisiana was ranked as one of the most [small business](276495.md) friendly states.[^cite_note-215] In 2025, 99.5% of businesses in Louisiana were small businesses and employed 54.1% of the state's work force.[^cite_note-216]&#x20;

Louisiana's principal agricultural products include seafood (it is the biggest producer of [crawfish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crawfish) in the world, supplying approximately 90%), cotton, [soybeans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soybean), cattle, [sugarcane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sugarcane), poultry and eggs, dairy products, and rice. Among its energy and other industries, chemical products, petroleum and coal products, processed foods, transportation equipment, and paper products have contributed to a significant portion of the state's GSP. Tourism and gaming are also important elements in the economy, especially in Greater New Orleans.[^cite_note-217]&#x20;

In 2004, the [Port of South Louisiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_South_Louisiana), on the [Mississippi River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mississippi_River) between New Orleans and [Baton Rouge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baton_Rouge), was the largest volume shipping port in the [Western Hemisphere](73162.md) and 4th largest in the world, and the largest [bulk cargo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bulk_cargo) port in the U.S.[^cite_note-218] The Port of South Louisiana continued to be the busiest port by tonnage in the U.S. to 2018.[^cite_note-219] South Louisiana was number 15 among world ports in 2016.[^cite_note-220]&#x20;



Tabasco varieties produced in Louisiana

[New Orleans](53842.md), [Shreveport](150473.md), and [Baton Rouge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baton_Rouge,_Louisiana) are home to a thriving film industry.[^cite_note-221] State financial incentives since 2002 and aggressive promotion have given Louisiana the nickname "Hollywood South". Because of its distinctive culture within the United States, only [Alaska](624.md) is Louisiana's rival in popularity as a setting for reality television programs.[^cite_note-robertson20130516-222] In late 2007 and early 2008, a 300,000-square-foot (28,000 m<sup>2</sup>) film studio was scheduled to open in [Tremé](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trem%C3%A9), with state-of-the-art production facilities, and a film training institute.[^cite_note-223] [Tabasco sauce](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tabasco_sauce), which is marketed by one of the United States' biggest producers of hot sauce, the [McIlhenny Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McIlhenny_Company), originated on [Avery Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avery_Island).[^cite_note-224]&#x20;

In 2025, Louisiana's [gross state product](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gross_regional_domestic_product) was \$340 billion.[^cite_note-apps.bea.gov-225] Louisiana's [per capita personal income](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Per_capita_personal_income_in_the_United_States) was \$63,940 in 2025.[^cite_note-apps.bea.gov-225] In 2019, the median household income was \$51,073, while the national average was \$65,712.[^cite_note-226] In August 2025, Louisiana's unemployment rate was 4.4%.[^cite_note-227]&#x20;

Louisiana has three personal [income tax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Income_tax) brackets, ranging from 2% to 6%. The state [sales tax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sales_tax) rate is 5% (effective January 1, 2025), and [parishes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_parishes_in_Louisiana) can levy additional sales tax on top of this.[^cite_note-228] Louisiana has a [use tax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Use_tax), which includes 4% to be distributed to local governments. Property taxes are assessed and collected at the local level. Louisiana is a subsidized state, and Louisiana taxpayers receive more federal funding per dollar of federal taxes paid compared to the average state.[^cite_note-229]&#x20;

Per dollar of federal tax collected in 2005, Louisiana citizens received approximately \$1.78 in the way of federal spending. This ranks the state fourth highest nationally and represents a rise from 1995 when Louisiana received \$1.35 per dollar of taxes in federal spending (ranked seventh nationally). Neighboring states and the amount of federal spending received per dollar of federal tax collected were: Texas (\$0.94), Arkansas (\$1.41), and Mississippi (\$2.02). Federal spending in 2005 and subsequent years since has been exceptionally high due to the recovery from Hurricane Katrina. Nearly a third of Louisianans are Medicaid recipients.[^cite_note-230]&#x20;

## Culture

Main articles: [Culture of Louisiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Louisiana), [Literature of Louisiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Literature_of_Louisiana), and [Music of Louisiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_Louisiana)

Louisiana is home to many cultures; especially notable are the distinct cultures of the [Louisiana Creoles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louisiana_Creoles) and [Cajuns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cajuns), descendants of French and Spanish settlers in colonial Louisiana.&#x20;

### African culture

The French colony of *La Louisiane* struggled for decades to survive. Conditions were harsh, the climate and soil were unsuitable for certain crops the colonists knew, and they suffered from regional tropical diseases. Both colonists and the slaves they imported had high mortality rates. The settlers kept importing slaves, which resulted in a high proportion of native Africans from West Africa, who continued to practice their culture in new surroundings. As described by historian [Gwendolyn Midlo Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gwendolyn_Midlo_Hall), they developed a marked Afro-Creole culture in the colonial era.[^cite_note-231][^cite_note-232]&#x20;

At the turn of the 18th century and in the early 1800s, New Orleans received a major influx of White and mixed-race refugees fleeing the violence of the [Haitian Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haitian_Revolution), many of whom brought their slaves with them.[^cite_note-233] This added another infusion of African culture to the city, as more slaves in [Saint-Domingue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint-Domingue) were from Africa than in the United States. They strongly influenced the African-American culture of the city in terms of dance, music and religious practices.&#x20;

### Creole culture



Typical dishes of [Louisiana Creole cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louisiana_Creole_cuisine)

[Creole](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louisiana_Creole_people) culture is an amalgamation of French, African, Spanish (and other European), and Native American cultures.[^cite_note-234] Creole comes from the Portuguese word *crioulo*; originally it referred to a colonist of European (specifically French) descent who was born in the New World, in comparison to immigrants from France.[^cite_note-235] The oldest Louisiana manuscript to use the word "Creole", from 1782, applied it to a slave born in the French colony.[^cite_note-236] But originally it referred more generally to the French colonists born in Louisiana.&#x20;

Over time, there developed in the French colony a relatively large group of [Creoles of Color](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Creoles_of_Color) (*gens de couleur libres*), who were primarily descended from African slave women and French men (later other Europeans became part of the mix, as well as some Native Americans). Often the French would free their concubines and [mixed-race](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mixed-race) children, and pass on social capital to them.[^cite_note-237] They might educate sons in France, for instance, and help them enter the French Army. They also settled capital or property on their mistresses and children. The free people of color gained more rights in the colony and sometimes education; they generally spoke French and were Roman Catholic. Many became artisans and property owners. Over time, the term "Creole" became associated with this class of Creoles of color, many of whom achieved freedom long before the American Civil War.&#x20;

Wealthy French Creoles generally maintained town houses in [New Orleans](53842.md) as well as houses on their large sugar plantations outside town along the Mississippi River. New Orleans had the largest population of free people of color in the region; they could find work there and created their own culture, marrying among themselves for decades.&#x20;

### Acadian culture

The ancestors of [Cajuns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cajun) immigrated mostly from west central France to New France, where they settled in the Atlantic provinces of [New Brunswick](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Brunswick), Nova Scotia and [Prince Edward Island](23071.md), known originally as the French colony of [Acadia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acadia). After the British defeated France in the [French and Indian War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_and_Indian_War) ([Seven Years' War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seven_Years%27_War)) in 1763, France ceded its territory east of the Mississippi River to Britain. After the Acadians refused to swear an oath of loyalty to the British Crown, they were [expelled from Acadia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Expulsion_of_the_Acadians), and made their way to places such as France, Britain, and New England.[^cite_note-238]&#x20;

Other Acadians covertly remained in [British North America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_America) or moved to [New Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Spain). Many Acadians settled in southern Louisiana in the region around [Lafayette](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lafayette,_Louisiana) and the LaFourche Bayou country. They developed a distinct rural culture there, different from the French Creole colonists of New Orleans. Intermarrying with others in the area, they developed what was called Cajun music, cuisine and culture.&#x20;

### Isleño culture

Main article: [Isleños (Louisiana)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isle%C3%B1os_\(Louisiana\))



El Museo de los Isleños (Isleño Museum) in [Saint Bernard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Bernard,_Louisiana)

A third distinct culture in Louisiana is that of the Isleños. Its members are descendants of colonists from the [Canary Islands](5717.md) who settled in [Spanish Louisiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_Louisiana) between 1778 and 1783 and intermarried with other communities such as [Frenchmen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_people), [Acadians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acadians), [Creoles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louisiana_Creole_people), [Spaniards](963152.md), and other groups, mainly through the 19th and early 20th centuries.&#x20;

In Louisiana, the Isleños originally settled in four communities which included Galveztown, Valenzuela, Barataria, and San Bernardo. The large migration of [Acadian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acadian) refugees to [Bayou Lafourche](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bayou_Lafourche) led to the rapid gallicization of the Valenzuela community while the community of San Bernardo ([Saint Bernard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Bernard_Parish,_Louisiana)) was able to preserve much of its unique culture and language into the 21st century. The transmission of Spanish and other customs has completely halted in St. Bernard with those having competency in Spanish being octogenarians.[^cite_note-\:02-239]&#x20;

Through the centuries, the various Isleño communities of Louisiana have kept alive different elements of their Canary Islander heritage while also adopting and building upon the customs and traditions of the communities that surround them. Today two heritage associates exist for the communities: Los Isleños Heritage and Cultural Society of St. Bernard as well as the Canary Islanders Heritage Society of Louisiana. The [Fiesta de los Isleños](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isle%C3%B1os_Fiesta) is celebrated annually in St. Bernard Parish which features heritage performances from local groups and the Canary Islands.[^cite_note-240]&#x20;

## Education

Further information: [List of school districts in Louisiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_school_districts_in_Louisiana), [List of high schools in Louisiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_high_schools_in_Louisiana), [List of colleges and universities in Louisiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_colleges_and_universities_in_Louisiana), and [French immersion in Louisiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_immersion#United_States)



Aerial view of Louisiana State University's flagship campus

Despite ranking as the third-least educated state as of 2023, preceded by Mississippi and [West Virginia](32905.md),[^cite_note-\:10-22] Louisiana is home to over 40 public and private [colleges and universities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_colleges_and_universities_in_Louisiana) including: [Louisiana State University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louisiana_State_University) in Baton Rouge; [Louisiana Tech University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louisiana_Tech_University) in Ruston, the [University of Louisiana at Lafayette](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Louisiana_at_Lafayette) in Lafayette; and [Tulane University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tulane_University) in New Orleans. Louisiana State University is the largest and most comprehensive university in the state.[^cite_note-241] Louisiana Tech University is one of the most well regarded public universities in the state.[^cite_note-242][^cite_note-243][^cite_note-244] The University of Louisiana at Lafayette, the second-largest institution in the state, became the third university in the state to achieve [*R1: Doctoral universities – very high research activity*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carnegie_Classification_of_Institutions_of_Higher_Education) status in December 2021.[^cite_note-245] Tulane University is a major private research university and the wealthiest university in Louisiana with an endowment over \$2 billion in 2023.[^cite_note-246] Tulane is also highly regarded for its academics nationwide, consistently ranked in the top 100 on *[U.S. News & World Report](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._News_%26_World_Report)'s* [list of best national universities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._News_%26_World_Report_Best_Colleges_Ranking).[^cite_note-247]&#x20;

Louisiana's two largest [historically black colleges and universities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historically_black_colleges_and_universities) (HBCUs) are [Southern University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_University) in Baton Rouge and [Grambling State University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grambling_State_University) in Grambling. Both these [Southwestern Athletic Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southwestern_Athletic_Conference) (SWAC) schools compete against each other in football annually in the much anticipated and nationally televised [Bayou Classic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bayou_Classic) during Thanksgiving weekend in the [Superdome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mercedes-Benz_Superdome).[^cite_note-248]&#x20;

While Louisiana has historically struggled to improve its test scores nationwide, recent [improvements in policy and practice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mississippi_Miracle) have led to substantial growth.[^cite_note-249] In 2000, of all of the states, Louisiana had the highest percentage of students in private schools. Danielle Dreilinger of *[The Times Picayune](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Times_Picayune)* wrote in 2014 that "Louisiana parents have a national reputation for favoring private schools."[^cite_note-Dreilingerenrollmentfalls-250] The number of students in enrolled in private schools in Louisiana declined by 9% from c. 2000–2005 until 2014, due to the proliferation of [charter schools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charter_schools), the [Great Recession](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Recession) and [Hurricane Katrina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hurricane_Katrina). Ten parishes in the Baton Rouge and New Orleans area had a combined 17% decline in private school enrollment in that period. This prompted private schools to lobby for [school vouchers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/School_vouchers).[^cite_note-Dreilingerenrollmentfalls-250]&#x20;

Louisiana's school voucher program is known as the Louisiana Scholarship Program. It was available in the New Orleans area beginning in 2008 and in the rest of the state beginning in 2012.[^cite_note-251] In 2013, the number of students using [school vouchers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/School_vouchers) to attend private schools was 6,751, and for 2014 it was projected to exceed 8,800.[^cite_note-252] As per a ruling from [Ivan Lemelle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ivan_Lemelle), a U.S. district judge, the federal government has the right to review the charter school placements to ensure they do not further racial segregation.[^cite_note-253]&#x20;

## Transportation

The [Louisiana Department of Transportation and Development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louisiana_Department_of_Transportation_and_Development) is the state government organization in charge of maintaining [public transportation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_transportation), roadways, bridges, [canals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canal), select [levees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Levee), floodplain management, port facilities, commercial vehicles, and aviation which includes 69 airports.&#x20;

In 2022, Louisiana ranked 5th highest for fatal crashes in the US with a rate of 19.7 deaths per 100,000 population.[^cite_note-254]&#x20;

Six [Class I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Class_I_railroad) freight railroads operate in Louisiana: [BNSF](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BNSF), [Canadian National](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadian_National), [CPKC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CPKC), [CSX](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CSX), [Norfolk Southern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Norfolk_Southern) and [Union Pacific](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_Pacific). A number of Class II and Class III railroads also carry freight.&#x20;

[Amtrak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amtrak), the national passenger railroad, operates three long-distance rail routes through Louisiana. All three originate at [New Orleans Union Passenger Terminal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Orleans_Union_Passenger_Terminal). The *[Crescent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crescent_\(train\))* serves [Slidell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slidell_station) then runs northeast to [New York](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_Penn_Station) via [Birmingham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Birmingham_station_\(Alabama\)), [Atlanta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peachtree_station), [Charlotte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charlotte_station_\(Amtrak\)), and [Washington, D.C.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Union_Station) The *[City of New Orleans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_of_New_Orleans)* stops at [Hammond](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hammond_station_\(Louisiana\)) before continuing north to [Chicago](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicago_Union_Station) by way of [Jackson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_Station_\(Jackson,_Mississippi\)) and [Memphis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Memphis_Central_Station). The *[Sunset Limited](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunset_Limited)* serves [Schriever](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schriever_station), [New Iberia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Iberia_station), [Lafayette](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lafayette_station_\(Indiana\)), and [Lake Charles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_Charles_station) on its route west to [Los Angeles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Los_Angeles_Union_Station) via [Houston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Houston_station_\(Texas\)), [San Antonio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Antonio_station_\(Texas\)), [El Paso](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_Depot_\(El_Paso\)), and [Tucson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tucson_station). Before [Hurricane Katrina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hurricane_Katrina), the *Sunset Limited* ran as far east as [Orlando](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orlando_Health/Amtrak_station).&#x20;

### Mass transit



A streetcar on the [St. Charles Avenue Line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Streetcars_in_New_Orleans) in New Orleans

Predominantly serving [New Orleans](53842.md), the [New Orleans Regional Transit Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Orleans_Regional_Transit_Authority) is the largest transit agency in the state. Other transit organizations are [St. Bernard Urban Rapid Transit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Bernard_Urban_Rapid_Transit), [Jefferson Transit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jefferson_Transit_\(Louisiana\)), [Capital Area Transit System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capital_Area_Transit_System), [Lafayette Transit System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lafayette_Transit_System), [Shreveport Area Transit System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shreveport_Area_Transit_System), and [Monroe Transit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monroe_Transit), among others.&#x20;

The Louisiana Transportation Authority (under the [Louisiana Department of Transportation and Development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louisiana_Department_of_Transportation_and_Development)) was created in 2001 to "promote, plan, finance, develop, construct, control, regulate, operate and maintain any tollway or transitway to be constructed within its jurisdiction. Development, construction, improvement, expansion, and maintenance of an efficient, safe, and well-maintained [intermodal transportation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intermodal_passenger_transport) system is essential to promote Louisiana's economic growth and the ability of Louisiana's business and industry to compete in regional, national, and global markets and to provide a high quality of life for the people of Louisiana."[^cite_note-255]&#x20;

### Air

See also: [List of airports in Louisiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_airports_in_Louisiana)

[Louis Armstrong New Orleans International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louis_Armstrong_New_Orleans_International_Airport) (MSY) is the busiest airport in Louisiana by an order of magnitude. It is also the second lowest-lying international airport in the world, at just 4.5 feet (1.4 m) above sea level. There are six other [primary airports](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FAA_airport_categories) in the state: [Baton Rouge Metropolitan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baton_Rouge_Metropolitan_Airport), [Shreveport Regional](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shreveport_Regional_Airport), [Lafayette Regional](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lafayette_Regional_Airport), [Alexandria International](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexandria_International_Airport_\(Louisiana\)), [Monroe Regional](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monroe_Regional_Airport_\(Louisiana\)), and [Lake Charles Regional](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_Charles_Regional_Airport). A total of 69 public-use airports exist in Louisiana.[^cite_note-256]&#x20;

### Waterways



[Gulf Intracoastal Waterway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_Intracoastal_Waterway) near New Orleans

The [Gulf Intracoastal Waterway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_Intracoastal_Waterway) is an important means of transporting commercial goods such as petroleum and petroleum products, agricultural produce, building materials and manufactured goods. In 2018, the state sued the federal government to repair erosion along the waterway.[^cite_note-257]&#x20;

Further information: [List of Louisiana Governors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Louisiana_Governors), [Louisiana law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louisiana_law), and [Louisiana Constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louisiana_Constitution)



The [Louisiana State Capitol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louisiana_State_Capitol) in Baton Rouge, the [tallest state capitol building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heights_of_state_capitols) in the United States



The Louisiana Governor's Mansion

In 1849, the state moved the capital from New Orleans to [Baton Rouge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baton_Rouge). [Donaldsonville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donaldsonville,_Louisiana), [Opelousas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Opelousas,_Louisiana), and [Shreveport](150473.md) have briefly served as the seat of Louisiana state government. The [Louisiana State Capitol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louisiana_State_Capitol) and the [Louisiana Governor's Mansion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louisiana_Governor%27s_Mansion) are both located in Baton Rouge. The [Louisiana Supreme Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louisiana_Supreme_Court), however, did not move to Baton Rouge but remains headquartered in New Orleans.&#x20;

Louisiana has six [congressional districts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congressional_district) and is represented in the [U.S. House of Representatives](19468510.md) by four Republicans and two Democrats. Louisiana had eight votes in the [Electoral College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Electoral_College) for the [2020 election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_United_States_presidential_election_in_Louisiana).&#x20;

In a 2020 study, Louisiana was ranked as the 24th hardest state for citizens to vote in.[^cite_note-258] Louisiana has one of the most restrictive [abortion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abortion_in_Louisiana) laws in the United States.[^cite_note-259]&#x20;

The [Louisiana State Penitentiary, Angola](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louisiana_State_Penitentiary) is the largest [maximum-security prison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Types_of_US_federal_prisons) in the United States.[^cite_note-260]&#x20;

### Administrative divisions

Louisiana is divided into 64 [parishes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parish_\(administrative_division\)) (the equivalent of [counties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/County_\(United_States\)) in most other states).[^cite_note-nativeamericans-261]&#x20;

- [List of parishes in Louisiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_parishes_in_Louisiana)

- [Louisiana census statistical areas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louisiana_census_statistical_areas)

Most parishes have an elected government known as the Police Jury,[^cite_note-262] dating from the colonial days. It is the legislative and executive government of the parish, and is elected by the voters. Its members are called Jurors, and together they elect a president as their chairman.&#x20;

A more limited number of parishes operate under [home rule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Home_rule) charters, electing various forms of government. This include mayor–council, council–manager (in which the council hires a professional operating manager for the parish), and others.&#x20;

### Civil law

The Louisiana political and legal structure has maintained several elements from the times of French and Spanish governance. One is the use of the term "[parish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parish_\(Louisiana\))" (from the French: *paroisse*) in place of "[county](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/County_\(United_States\))" for administrative subdivision.[^cite_note-263] Another is the legal system of [civil law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_law_\(legal_system\)) based on French, German, and Spanish [legal codes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legal_code) and ultimately [Roman law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_law), as opposed to English [common law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Common_law).&#x20;

Louisiana's civil law system is what the majority of [sovereign states](23604120.md) in the world use, especially in Europe and its former colonies, excluding those that derive their legal systems from the [British Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Empire). However, it is incorrect to equate the [Louisiana Civil Code](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louisiana_Civil_Code) with the [Napoleonic Code](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Napoleonic_Code). Although the Napoleonic Code and Louisiana law draw from common legal roots, the Napoleonic Code was never in force in Louisiana, as it was enacted in 1804, after the United States had [purchased](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louisiana_Purchase) and annexed Louisiana in 1803.[^cite_note-264]&#x20;

The Louisiana Civil Code is the controlling authority on civil matters in the state and has been continuously revised and updated since its enactment in 1808. While some of the differences between the legal systems have been bridged due to the strong influence of common law tradition,[^cite_note-kinsellalaw-265] the civil law tradition is still deeply rooted in most aspects of Louisiana private law.[^cite_note-266] Thus property, contractual, business entities structure, much of civil procedure, and family law, as well as some aspects of criminal law, are based mostly on traditional Roman legal thinking.&#x20;

### Marriage

In 1997, Louisiana became the first state to offer the option of a traditional marriage or a [covenant marriage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Covenant_marriage).[^cite_note-267] In a covenant marriage, the couple waives their right to a "no-fault" divorce after six months of separation, which is available in a traditional marriage. To divorce under a covenant marriage, a couple must demonstrate cause. Marriages between ascendants and descendants, and marriages between collaterals within the fourth degree (i.e., siblings, aunt and nephew, uncle and niece, first cousins) are prohibited.[^cite_note-268] [Same-sex marriages](92656.md) were prohibited by statute,[^cite_note-269][^cite_note-270] but the [U.S. Supreme Court](31737.md) declared such bans unconstitutional in 2015 in *[Obergefell v. Hodges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Obergefell_v._Hodges)*. [Same-sex marriages are now performed statewide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBT_rights_in_Louisiana). Louisiana is a [community property](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Community_property) state.[^cite_note-271]&#x20;

From 1898 to 1965, a period when Louisiana had effectively [disfranchised](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disfranchisement_after_the_Civil_War) most African Americans and many poor whites by provisions of a new constitution,[^cite_note-GWUweb-273] this was essentially a [one-party state](102459.md) dominated by white Democrats. Elites had control in the early 20th century, before populist [Huey Long](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Huey_Long) came to power as governor.[^cite_note-Cashman-274] In multiple acts of resistance, blacks left behind the segregation, violence and oppression of the state and moved out to seek better opportunities in northern and western industrial cities during the [Great Migrations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Migration_\(African_American\)) of 1910–1970, markedly reducing their proportion of population in Louisiana. The franchise for whites was expanded somewhat during these decades, but blacks remained essentially disfranchised until after the [civil rights movement](49001.md) of the mid-20th century, gaining enforcement of their constitutional rights through passage by Congress of the [Voting Rights Act of 1965](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voting_Rights_Act_of_1965).&#x20;

Since the 1960s, when civil rights legislation was passed under President [Lyndon Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lyndon_Johnson) to protect voting and civil rights, most African Americans in the state have affiliated with the Democratic Party. In the same years, many white social conservatives have moved to support Republican Party candidates in national, gubernatorial and statewide elections. In 2004, [David Vitter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Vitter) was the first Republican in Louisiana to be popularly elected as a U.S. senator.[^cite_note-275] The previous Republican senator, [John S. Harris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_S._Harris), who took office in 1868 during Reconstruction, was chosen by the state legislature under the rules of the 19th century.&#x20;

Louisiana is unique among U.S. states in using a system for its state and local elections similar to that of modern France. All candidates, regardless of party affiliation, run in a [nonpartisan blanket primary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nonpartisan_blanket_primary) (or "jungle primary") on [Election Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Election_Day_\(United_States\)).[^cite_note-276] If no candidate has more than 50% of the vote, the two candidates with the highest vote totals compete in a runoff election approximately one month later. This run-off method does not take into account party identification; therefore, it is not uncommon for a Democrat to be in a runoff with a fellow Democrat or a Republican to be in a runoff with a fellow Republican.&#x20;

Congressional races have also been held under the jungle primary system. All other states (except [Washington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Initiative_872_\(2004\)), [California](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/California_Proposition_14_\(2010\)), and [Maine](19977.md)) use single-party primaries followed by a general election between party candidates, each conducted by either a [plurality voting system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plurality_voting_system) or [runoff voting](25932.md), to elect senators, representatives, and statewide officials. Between 2008 and 2010, federal [congressional](31756.md) elections were run under a closed primary system—limited to registered party members. However, on the passage of House Bill 292, Louisiana again adopted a nonpartisan blanket primary for its federal congressional elections.&#x20;

Louisiana has six seats in the [U.S. House of Representatives](19468510.md), five of which are currently held by Republicans and one by a Democrat. Though the state historically flips between Republican and Democratic governors, Louisiana is not classified as a [swing state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swing_state) in presidential elections, and has it has been considered a [Republican Party stronghold](32070.md) during the 21st century.[^cite_note-277] Louisiana's incumbent governor is [Republican](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republican_Party_of_Louisiana) [Jeff Landry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jeff_Landry), and the state's two U.S. senators are [Bill Cassidy](9386382.md) (R) and [John Neely Kennedy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Neely_Kennedy) (R).&#x20;

### Law enforcement

See also: [List of law enforcement agencies in Louisiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_law_enforcement_agencies_in_Louisiana)

Louisiana's statewide police force is the [Louisiana State Police](5875801.md). In 1988, the Criminal Investigation Bureau was reorganized.[^cite_note-279] Its troopers have statewide jurisdiction with power to enforce all laws of the state, including city and parish ordinances. Each year, they patrol over 12 million miles (19 million kilometres) of roadway and arrest about 10,000 impaired drivers. The State Police are primarily a traffic enforcement agency, with other sections that delve into trucking safety, narcotics enforcement, and gaming oversight.&#x20;



Mardi Gras celebrations in the Spanish Town section of Baton Rouge

The elected sheriff in each parish is its chief law enforcement officer. They are the keepers of the local parish prisons, which house felony and misdemeanor prisoners. They are the primary criminal patrol and first responder agency in all matters criminal and civil. They are also the official tax collectors in each parish. The sheriffs are responsible for general law enforcement in their respective parishes, with the exception of Orleans Parish where this falls to the New Orleans Police Department. Before 2010, Orleans Parish was the only parish to have two sheriff's offices, with a different elected sheriff overseeing civil and criminal matters. In 2006, a bill was passed which eventually consolidated the two sheriff's departments into one parish sheriff responsible for both.[^cite_note-280]&#x20;

In 2015, Louisiana had a higher murder rate (10.3 per 100,000) than any other state in the country for the 27th straight year. Louisiana is the only state with an annual average murder rate (13.6 per 100,000) at least twice as high as the U.S. annual average (6.6 per 100,000) during that period, according to Bureau of Justice Statistics from FBI Uniform Crime Reports. In a different kind of criminal activity, the *[Chicago Tribune](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicago_Tribune)* reports that Louisiana is the most corrupt state in the United States.[^cite_note-281]&#x20;

According to a 2012 article in *[The Times Picayune](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Times_Picayune)*, Louisiana is the prison capital of the world. Many [for-profit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Private_prison) private prisons and sheriff-owned prisons have been built and operate here. Louisiana's incarceration rate is nearly five times Iran's, 13 times China's and 20 times Germany's. Minorities are incarcerated at rates disproportionate to their share of the state's population.[^cite_note-282] There are more people serving life sentences without parole in Louisiana than in Texas, Tennessee, Arkansas, Alabama and Mississippi combined.[^cite_note-283]&#x20;

The [New Orleans Police Department](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Orleans_Police_Department) began a [sanctuary policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanctuary_city) to "no longer cooperate with federal immigration enforcement" beginning on February 28, 2016.[^cite_note-284]&#x20;

On June 19, 2024, [Jeff Landry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jeff_Landry) signed a bill to officially require that the [Ten Commandments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ten_Commandments) be displayed in every classroom in public schools and colleges.[^cite_note-285] In November 2024, the law was overturned by a federal court.[^cite_note-286]&#x20;

### Judiciary

The [judiciary of Louisiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judiciary_of_Louisiana) is defined under the [constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Louisiana) and [law of Louisiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Law_of_Louisiana) and comprises the [Louisiana Supreme Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louisiana_Supreme_Court), the [Louisiana Circuit Courts of Appeal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louisiana_Circuit_Courts_of_Appeal), the district courts, the Justice of the Peace courts, the mayor's courts, the city courts, and the parish courts. The chief justice of the Louisiana Supreme Court is the chief administrator of the judiciary. Its administration is aided by the Judiciary Commission of Louisiana, the [Louisiana Attorney Disciplinary Board](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Louisiana_Attorney_Disciplinary_Board\&action=edit\&redlink=1), and the Judicial Council of the Supreme Court of Louisiana.&#x20;

## National Guard

Louisiana has more than 9,000 soldiers in the [Louisiana Army National Guard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louisiana_Army_National_Guard), including the [225th Engineer Brigade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/225th_Engineer_Brigade) and the [256th Infantry Brigade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/256th_Infantry_Brigade).[^cite_note-287] Both these units have served overseas during the [war on terror](13425800.md).[^cite_note-288][^cite_note-289] The [Louisiana Air National Guard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louisiana_Air_National_Guard) has more than 2,000 airmen, and its 159th Fighter Wing has likewise seen combat.[^cite_note-290]&#x20;

Training sites in the state include [Louisiana National Guard Training Center Pineville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louisiana_National_Guard_Training_Center_Pineville) near [Pineville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pineville,_LA), Camp Villere near [Slidell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slidell,_LA), Camp Minden near [Minden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minden,_LA), [England Air Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/England_Air_Park) (formerly [England Air Force Base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/England_Air_Force_Base)) near [Alexandria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexandria,_LA), Gillis Long Center near [Carville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carville,_LA), and [Jackson Barracks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jackson_Barracks) in [New Orleans](53842.md).&#x20;

## Sports

See also: [List of sports teams in Louisiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_sports_teams_in_Louisiana) and [Sports in New Orleans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sports_in_New_Orleans)



[Caesars Superdome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mercedes-Benz_Superdome) and [Smoothie King Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smoothie_King_Center) in [New Orleans](53842.md).

Louisiana is the least populous state with more than one [major professional sports league](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Major_professional_sports_leagues_in_the_United_States_and_Canada) franchise: the [National Basketball Association](22093.md)'s [New Orleans Pelicans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Orleans_Pelicans) and the [National Football League](21211.md)'s [New Orleans Saints](21720.md).&#x20;

Louisiana has 12 collegiate [NCAA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NCAA) Division I programs, a high number given its population. The state has no NCAA Division II teams and only two NCAA Division III teams. As of 2019, the [LSU Tigers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LSU_Tigers) football team has won 12 [Southeastern Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southeastern_Conference) titles, six [Sugar Bowls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sugar_Bowl) and four national championships.[^cite_note-291]&#x20;

Each year New Orleans plays host to the [Bayou Classic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bayou_Classic), and the [New Orleans Bowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Orleans_Bowl) college football games, while Shreveport hosts the [Independence Bowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independence_Bowl). New Orleans has hosted the [Super Bowl](27718.md) a record eleven times,[^cite_note-292][^cite_note-293] as well as the [BCS National Championship Game](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BCS_National_Championship_Game), [NBA All-Star Game](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NBA_All-Star_Game) and [NCAA Men's Division I Basketball Championship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NCAA_Men%27s_Division_I_Basketball_Championship).&#x20;

The [Zurich Classic of New Orleans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zurich_Classic_of_New_Orleans), is a [PGA Tour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PGA_Tour) golf tournament held since 1938. The [Rock 'n' Roll Mardi Gras Marathon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rock_%27n%27_Roll_Mardi_Gras_Marathon) and [Crescent City Classic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crescent_City_Classic) are two road running competitions held at New Orleans.&#x20;

As of 2016, Louisiana was the birthplace of the most [NFL](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NFL) players per capita for the eighth year in a row.[^cite_note-294]&#x20;

[Louisiana State University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louisiana_State_University) has a total of 48 NCAA Division I national sports championships throughout the school's history. The most recent championship is the [college baseball](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NCAA_Baseball) championship in May 2025.&#x20;

[ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [978-0-19-533944-4](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/978-0-19-533944-4)

- [Yiannopoulos, A.N.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yiannopoulos,_A.N.), *The Civil Codes of Louisiana* (reprinted from Civil Law System: Louisiana and Comparative law, A Coursebook: Texts, Cases and Materials, 3d Edition; similar to version in preface to Louisiana Civil Code, ed. by Yiannopoulos)

- Rodolfo Batiza, "The Louisiana Civil Code of 1808: Its Actual Sources and Present Relevance", 46 *TUL. L. REV.* 4 (1971); Rodolfo Batiza, "Sources of the Civil Code of 1808, Facts and Speculation: A Rejoinder", 46 *TUL. L. REV.* 628 (1972); Robert A. Pascal, Sources of the Digest of 1808: A Reply to Professor Batiza, 46 TUL. L. REV. 603 (1972); Joseph M. Sweeney, Tournament of Scholars Over the Sources of the Civil Code of 1808,46 TUL. L. REV. 585 (1972).

- The standard history of the state, though only through the Civil War, is [Charles Gayarré](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Gayarr%C3%A9)'s *History of Louisiana* (various editions, culminating in 1866, 4 vols., with a posthumous and further expanded edition in 1885).

- A number of accounts by 17th- and 18th-century French explorers: Jean-Bernard Bossu, François-Marie Perrin du Lac, Pierre-François-Xavier de Charlevoix, Dumont (as published by Fr. Mascrier), Fr. [Louis Hennepin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louis_Hennepin), Lahontan, Louis Narcisse Baudry des Lozières, [Jean-Baptiste Bénard de la Harpe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean-Baptiste_B%C3%A9nard_de_la_Harpe), and Laval. In this group, the explorer [Antoine Simon Le Page du Pratz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antoine_Simon_Le_Page_du_Pratz) may be the first historian of Louisiana with his *Histoire de la Louisiane* (3 vols., Paris, 1758; 2 vols., London, 1763)

- [François Xavier Martin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fran%C3%A7ois_Xavier_Martin)'s *History of Louisiana* (2 vols., New Orleans, 1827–1829, later ed. by J. F. Condon, continued to 1861, New Orleans, 1882) is the first scholarly treatment of the subject, along with [François Barbé-Marbois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fran%C3%A7ois_Barb%C3%A9-Marbois)' *Histoire de la Louisiane et de la cession de colonie par la France aux Etats-Unis* (Paris, 1829; in English, Philadelphia, 1830).

- [Alcée Fortier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alc%C3%A9e_Fortier)'s *A History of Louisiana* (N.Y., 4 vols., 1904) is the most recent of the large-scale scholarly histories of the state.

- The official works of Albert Phelps and [Grace King](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grace_King), the publications of the Louisiana Historical Society and several works on [the history of New Orleans (q.v.)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_New_Orleans), among them those by Henry Rightor and John Smith Kendall provide background.

[^cite_note-207]: &#x20;Other Southern states have longstanding indigenous Catholic populations, and Florida's largely Catholic population of Cuban emigres has been influential since the 1960s. Yet, Louisiana is still unusual or exceptional in its extent of aboriginal Catholic settlement and influence. Among states in the [Deep South](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deep_South) (discounting [Florida's Panhandle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Florida_Panhandle) and much of Texas) the historic role of Catholicism in Louisiana is unparalleled and unique. Among the states of the Union, Louisiana's unique use of the term *parish* (French *la parouche* or "*la paroisse*") for *county* is rooted in the pre-statehood role of Catholic church parishes in the administration of government.

By the 1720s, free mixed-race Louisianans made up such a substantial part of the population that the Code Noir (laws governing race relations in Louisiana) spelled out the group's special place in colonial society. These Creoles of color, as they were known (gens de couleur libres in French, "free persons of color"), occupied a middle ground between whites and enslaved blacks. They commonly owned property, including slaves, and received formal educations, sometimes in Europe.
